## Stage 1

2026-09-28 — Repo stays private for now: no public GitHub (Brett). Local git only; a private remote is a separate call.

2026-09-28 — Stage 1 run on Brett's authorization. The spec's table reproduces to its stated precision (`results/stage1/stage1_reference_output.txt`): pooled figures over readings, language-level figures over speaker means then language means, sample SD. The Elicit audit's differing cells come from averaging each language directly over readings, which reproduces all of them exactly (39.247, 56.945, 8.448%, 10.645%, r = −0.834221, p = 3.14 × 10⁻⁵). The project keeps the spec's aggregation (speaker means first), since speakers are the sampled unit within a language.

2026-09-28 — Row count: the released analysis uses all 2288 rows (`InfoRate.Rmd` lines 197–249, no exclusion) and computes the row-level r over them (line 678), which gives −0.688. The paper's reported 2265 rows and −0.71 (per the spec) are not reproduced by the released code; resolve from the paper before quoting either.

## Source verification

2026-09-28 — Sources filed in `literature/` with markdown companions: Coupé et al. 2019 (Europe PMC XML, no PDF; the PMC PDF is behind a proof-of-work bot check, not attempted further), its supplement, Pellegrino et al. 2011, Pimentel et al. 2021, the SLE 2024 slides, Bergey & DeDeo 2024, Trott 2020. The supplement's data file S1 and script are byte-identical to the GitHub copies, so the GitHub versions are the published ones.

2026-09-28 — Every claim in the spec about the paper, Pellegrino et al. and the SLE talk checks out (`notes/source-verification.md`), with one correction: texts were translated from British English or French. Two discrepancies stay open and belong to the authors, not the spec: the paper states 2265 data points and a row-level Pearson r of −0.71, while the authors' own compiled report gives 2288 observations and −0.6880138 (its Spearman, −0.6999614, matches the paper's −0.70). Don't quote either paper figure without saying which.

2026-09-28 — The paper's model is less naive than "pseudoreplication" implies: the SR ~ ID fit keeps text, speaker and family random effects. What it drops is the language-level residual. Stage 8's reason stands in narrower form.

2026-09-28 — The 15 texts in all 17 languages are printed in SM Text S3. The Stage 3 unit-consistency check (pipeline syllable counts against NS per text) can use them without waiting on the authors, and the author email, if sent, narrows to the canonical transcriptions, the syllabified corpora or syllabifier, and the 2265 / −0.71 question.

2026-09-28 — Supplement licence: the paper says the data and code are "freely available under an open-source license in the Supplementary Materials and in the GitHub repository", but neither the GitHub repo nor the supplement archive names one. `data/ref/` stays gitignored.

## Working defaults from the spec review (2026-10-08)

Recommended in `notes/spec-review-2026-09-28.md`, adopted as working defaults so later stages have a plan. Not separately confirmed by Brett. Each is reversible until Stage 5 runs; Brett can veto by item number.

- Item 1: add rung **1b** (previous syllable across boundaries plus a word-initial flag) and give every higher rung the flag; keep rung 1 as the segmenter-free variant. This changes what the deliverable curve is, so it most needs Brett's explicit word.
- Item 2: held-out cross-entropy at every rung, including 0 and 1w; plug-in estimates only for the Stage 4 validation.
- Item 3: equal syllable-token training budgets across languages, out-of-vocabulary rates reported, the curve rerun at a second budget; learning-curve plateau checked for the cross-entropies at k = 3 and 4.
- Item 4: define R_k on SDs of logs and report v_k and r_k beside it.
- Item 5: compare rungs with a paired bootstrap over languages (R_k − R_1w within each resample).
- Item 7: compute segments per syllable both type- and token-weighted.
- Item 8: add log syllable-inventory size as a Stage 7 predictor.
- Item 9: exact-source Stage 4 check for ENG, DEU (WebCelex) and FRA (Lexique), subject to access and licence terms.

2026-10-08 — Item 1 settled (Brett): rung 1b runs in both forms, free (word-initial flag as context) and charged (boundaries predicted and their bits counted), alongside rung 1 with no boundaries. The three estimate H(S), H(S | B) and H(S, B), so their differences give I(S; B) and H(B | S) per language. Rationale: Gelman & Loken (2014), analyse all relevant comparisons. Decision owner: Brett; Claude had recommended the free form alone.

2026-10-08 — Working defaults confirmed (Brett): spec-review items 1–5 and 7–9 are now project decisions, with item 1 as amended above (rung 1, 1b-free and 1b-charged). Decision owner: Brett.

Second run on Wikipedia for all 17 languages, so one full run has a single source type. Stub filtering rule to be fixed and logged before any YUE entropy is computed. Decision owner: Brett.

## Prior-work check and disk budget (2026-10-08)

2026-10-08 — Spec review item 6, the outward check for an existing extension, done before any Stage 2 download (`notes/prior-work-check-2026-10-08.md`). Searched all 245 works that OpenAlex and Semantic Scholar list as citing Coupé et al. 2019 (list in `notes/prior-work-check-2026-10-08-citing-works.tsv`), plus seven web searches, with the queries and screens recorded in the note. No work found that recomputes the 17-language ID with cross-word or long-context models and re-evaluates rate compression. The claim covers the indexed citing works and those searches, not unindexed preprints or work that doesn't cite the 2019 paper.

2026-10-08 — Neighbours from the check (corpus awareness, outward). Oh & Pellegrino (2023, *Studies in Language* 47(4), Word Information Density on 47 languages, a reported trade-off between word-internal and across-word information): **deferred**, to be read in full before Stage 5, since it bears on the step from rung 1w to 1b; the repository copy sits behind a bot check, so Brett's browser is the route. Koplenig et al. (2025, *PLOS Complex Systems*, entropy rate against text length in 2,000+ written languages): **deferred**, as a framing neighbour for the write-up. The authors' SLE 2024 measure (GPT-2 surprisal of English translations): **deliberately distinguished**, because it measures the translations' content, not each language's own syllable sequence, which is what this project's ladder conditions on.

2026-10-08 — Disk budget (Brett: don't plan to max out the drive). Stage 2 never downloads a whole release: OpenSubtitles documents are fetched one at a time by HTTP range request from the OPUS raw zips, and each is normalised and stored as compressed text before the next is fetched, with no raw XML kept. Ceiling for the project's `data/`: 10 GB. Every fetch script checks free space first and stops if it would fall below 40 GB. Working default; Brett can change either number.

## Stage 2: OpenSubtitles sampling and normalisation (2026-10-08, before any fetch)

Logged before the fetcher runs. Brett chose to run everything locally (2026-10-08). The filters will be checked on Basque by reading what they reject before rollout; any revision made then is dated and marked **[post hoc, filters]**. That's post hoc with respect to the cleaning, not to any entropy, since none will have been computed.

2026-10-08 — Source: OPUS OpenSubtitles v2024, `raw/<code>.zip` (untokenised sentences in XML, one file per subtitle upload), for the 16 languages other than YUE. Each zip's URL, ETag, Last-Modified and size go in `results/stage2/manifest/opensubtitles_zips.tsv`. Members are fetched singly by HTTP range request and checked against the zip's CRC32. No zip is downloaded whole, and no raw XML is kept.

2026-10-08 — Unit: the folder above each file (`<year>/<id>/`). A film's folder is its IMDb id. A TV episode's folder is `<episode>_<series>_<season>_<episode no.>`, and its group is the series id, so the Stage 5 split by document keeps every episode of a series on one side. One subtitle file per folder: the lowest-numbered file of at least 10,000 bytes (a content-blind rule, set from the zip directory, which also skips the empty files found in `ja`); if it fails the document filters, the next one, at most three tries.

2026-10-08 — Sample: folders shuffled with seed 20261008 and taken in that order until the language's normalised text reaches 200 MB of UTF-8, or the folders run out. Basque (1,137 folders) and Catalan (1,602) are taken whole. Reason for 200 MB: Basque's whole release is about 40–50 MB of text (estimate), the training budget can't exceed the smallest language's syllable count, and 200 MB stays above Basque's syllable count even in Thai script, where bytes per syllable are highest. Sixteen languages at 200 MB fit the 10 GB ceiling with room for Wikipedia and the syllabified files. Output: `data/raw/opensubtitles/<LANG>.tsv.gz` (group, folder, file, line, text); per-document manifest with counts per filter in `results/stage2/manifest/`.

2026-10-08 — A line is one OPUS `<s>` sentence. N-gram contexts don't cross lines (the standard sentence-level setup for k ≤ 4); the optional neural rung may use whole documents.

2026-10-08 — Document filters: drop if `<machine_translated>` is 1; drop if OPUS's `<confidence>` for the language is present and below 0.9; drop if fewer than 50 lines survive; for Latin-script languages, drop if more than 20% of lines fail the letter check below (that rate signals a mis-encoded file).

2026-10-08 — Line normalisation, in order:
1. Text of the `<s>` element with `<time>` removed; NFKC; Unicode format characters (category Cf: direction marks, zero-width spaces, soft hyphens) removed; whitespace collapsed.
2. Encoding repairs where the wrong code page left letters foreign to the orthography: SRP è æ ð → č ć đ (and capitals), cp1250 read as cp1252, seen in the sample; TUR ý þ ð → ı ş ğ (and capitals); HUN õ û → ő ű (and capitals). Then SRP Cyrillic transliterated to Latin. The authors' Serbian texts are in Latin script, and Cyrillic to Latin is deterministic.
3. Markup removed: `<...>`, `{...}` (ASS and MicroDVD codes), backslash override codes, and bracketed or parenthesised text (sound cues, translator notes).
4. Line dropped if it contains ♪ ♫ ♬ or # (song lyrics).
5. Leading speaker labels in capitals followed by a colon removed (Latin scripts); leading dashes and dash turn separators removed.
6. Line dropped if it contains a URL, domain or e-mail address; within the first and last 20 lines of a document, also dropped if it contains a credit word (subtitle, translation, sync and their equivalents in the 16 languages).
7. Line dropped if it contains a digit. Spoken forms of numbers vary and would need a converter per language; the share dropped is reported. The read texts' digits are a separate Stage 3 conversion.
8. Letter check: line dropped if any letter is outside the language's inventory (for Latin scripts, a–z plus that orthography's letters, with acute vowels allowed in EUS for Spanish names; JPN kana and kanji; CMN Han only; KOR Hangul only; THA Thai only). This removes Latin-letter lines from the CJK, Korean and Thai files, including the English half of bilingual Chinese subtitles.
9. Latin-script languages other than ENG: line dropped if `lingua-language-detector` 2.2.0, comparing only the target language and English, gives English 0.9 or more (lingua's Croatian model stands in for Latin-script Serbian). English is the main contaminant (untranslated lines).
10. Line dropped if no letters remain.

2026-10-08 — Known gap: OpenSubtitles `sr` may include Bosnian, Croatian or Montenegrin uploads. No variety filter is applied; the share of ijekavian forms may be measured later.

2026-10-08 — Environment: `.venv` (Python 3.12, uv) with `lingua-language-detector` 2.2.0, checked on PyPI 2026-10-08.

2026-10-08 — Correction to the line entry above. Whether n-gram contexts stop at each subtitle line is a Stage 5 modelling choice, and it was logged as settled without Brett's word (a side agent flagged it). Subtitle lines are short, so a line-bounded model leaves many syllables with truncated context at k = 3 and 4, and differences in typical line length between languages could move R_k on their own. Settled under Brett's standing instruction "always ask, what would Gelman do?" (2026-10-08): **run both and report both.** Every rung from k = 1 up is estimated twice, once bounded at each line (each subtitle sentence, each Wikipedia paragraph) and once running across lines within a document. The cross-line variant is listed first, because the read texts behind SR are continuous passages; neither is chosen after seeing results. Stage 2 keeps every document's lines in order with their original OPUS sentence positions, so gaps left by dropped sentences are visible and both variants can be built from the same files. Rationale: Gelman & Loken (2014), analyse all relevant comparisons. Mean line length per language goes in the Stage 2 summary.

2026-10-08 — Standing heuristic (Brett): at every analytic fork, ask what Gelman would do. In practice: where a defensible choice could move the result, run the alternatives and report them side by side rather than picking one; fix in advance which comparisons are reported; prefer partial pooling to dropping or splitting groups; check models against data (posterior predictive checks) rather than against thresholds.

2026-10-08 — **[post hoc, filters]** Revisions after reading what the filters rejected on Basque (`data/interim/stage2_rejects/EUS.tsv`, 300-line samples per filter), before any entropy was computed. (1) The English filter dropped 11,117 Basque lines, and in the sample most were names alone ("Josh!", "Alice!") or Basque lines containing an English name ("Bai, Billy.", "Tony Bennett zalea zen."). Names stay in every other language's text, so the filter was removing them only in the Latin-script languages. It now tests only lines of four or more words that contain at least one English function word that isn't also common in the 16 languages (`EN_WORDS` in `src/stage2_opensubtitles.py`; *he, so, was, will, on, me, i, to, do, a, no* are left out), with the same lingua threshold. On eight sampled lines it drops the three English ones and keeps the five Basque ones. (2) Control characters (category Cc, cp1252 punctuation read as C1 codes, 75 in kept Basque text) are removed with the format characters. (3) ¶ counts as a song marker, like ♪ (219 in kept Basque text). (4) A line containing U+FFFD (a decoding failure) fails the letter check. The rest held up: the credit filter caught the Basque Government's subtitle credits, the letter filter (824 lines) caught foreign names with diacritics and mojibake, the digit filter (23,288 lines, 2.0%) caught numbers.

2026-10-08 — **[post hoc, filters]** The OPUS confidence filter isn't applied to Serbian. In the first 30 sampled `sr` folders, 28 files carried `<confidence>0.5</confidence>`, which is OPUS's identifier splitting between Serbian and Croatian or Bosnian, not a sign of another language, and the filter was dropping nearly all of them. The other 15 languages keep the filter; their confidence distributions are checked in the manifests. Serbian files whose text was double-encoded UTF-8 ("Ä†ao" for "Ćao") lose those lines to the letter check, or the whole file to the 20% letter-rate rule.

## Stage 2: Wikipedia source (2026-10-08)

2026-10-08 — Wikipedia source for the YUE main-run corpus and the all-17 second run: Hugging Face `wikimedia/wikipedia`, snapshot 20231101 (configs `20231101.<code>`: vi, eu, ca, de, en, fr, it, es, sr, ja, ko, zh for CMN, zh-yue for YUE, th, tr, fi, hu). Decision owner: Brett. Reason: plain text with page ids, cleaned the same way for all 17 languages, so no wikitext parser has to be chosen and validated. This amends the Cantonese entry's "`zh_yuewiki` dump": the snapshot is derived from the 2023-11-01 dumps, not the 2026-10-01 ones. Checks before sampling: whether rows are stored in page-id order (if so, sampling whole shards is biased by article age), and the mix of traditional and simplified script in `zh`. The stub filter is fixed and logged before any entropy, and applies to all 17.

2026-10-08 — Oh & Pellegrino (2023) read in full (`notes/prior-work-check-2026-10-08.md`). Adjudication updated from **deferred**: its measures are **deliberately distinguished** (a translation length ratio and a word-order compression index on Bible text, not syllable conditional entropy within each language); its result is **adopted** as the source of a directional expectation, stated here before any Stage 5 estimate. Expectation: from rung 1w to rung 1b (both forms) and on to k = 2, the isolating languages (VIE, CMN, YUE, THA) lose more bits per syllable than the agglutinative ones (FIN, HUN, TUR, EUS). It is reported as a comparison of the two groups' mean drops with a bootstrap interval over languages, not as a significance test. The spec's Stage 6 already names this contrast; this entry fixes its direction in advance. Koplenig et al. (2017, *PLoS ONE* 12(3): e0173614), cited there for the same trade-off on more than 1,000 languages: **deferred**, not in `literature/`.

2026-10-08 — Wikipedia stub rule approved as proposed (Brett; `notes/wikipedia-stub-rule-proposal.md`). For all 17 Wikipedia samples: prose lines only (ending in sentence-final punctuation; Thai gets its own line rule, fixed after inspecting Thai data and before any Thai entropy); an article is dropped if 0.5 or more of its prose 8-grams (digits masked, 1 in 4 kept by a stable hash) occur in at least 20 articles of the language's sample; every Wikipedia sample is also run at a 0.3 threshold and both are reported. Measured on YUE before approval: the 0.5 threshold drops 28% of articles and 4% of prose characters, leaving 23.5M. Decision owner: Brett.

2026-10-08 — **[post hoc, filters]** Revisions after reading the Thai and Japanese output, before any entropy. (1) NFKC decomposes Thai sara am (U+0E33) into nikhahit plus sara aa, so every ำ in the Thai sample came out as ํา ("ตําแหน่ง"); sara am is now recomposed after NFKC, matching the read texts and Thai tools. (2) Escaped HTML entities are unescaped before anything else, and remnants that lost their "&" (lrm; rlm; nbsp;) are removed: in Thai they made the letter check drop good lines ("ไม่ว่าลืมตาหรือหลับตา lrm;"), and in Serbian 634 of 7.3M kept lines carried "&apos;" and the like as fake letters. (3) A line with a private-use character (Unicode category Co) fails the letter check: legacy Thai fonts put shifted tone marks in U+F700–F71A (376 kept Thai lines, 1 Japanese), and mapping them back would need a sourced table. THA and SRP are re-run under the fixed code, and the partial KOR run, begun under the old code, is discarded rather than resumed. EUS, CAT and JPN had no entity remnants; JPN's one private-use line is left as is.

2026-10-08 — Corpus terms recorded in `config/languages.yaml`, **late**: the project rule is to check each licence before download, and the OpenSubtitles fetch began without it (side agent flagged it; the advisor had raised it earlier). OPUS OpenSubtitles v2024 names no licence; OPUS asks for a link to opensubtitles.org in reports and publications and a citation of Lison & Tiedemann (2016), and takes material down on copyright claims. Consequence: the cleaned subtitle text is never redistributed, and the data statement offers manifests and code to rebuild the sample. Wikipedia 20231101 (Hugging Face) is CC BY-SA 3.0 and GFDL, redistributable with attribution and share-alike. Nothing found forbids the research use, so the fetch continues. The paper needs the opensubtitles.org link and the Lison & Tiedemann citation.

2026-10-08 — Wikipedia fetcher (`src/stage2_wikipedia.py`), implementing the approved stub rule. Unit choices that follow from it: a Wikipedia "line" for the n-gram models is a sentence within a prose paragraph (split after 。！？!? in CMN, YUE and JPN, after . ! ? … plus a space elsewhere, but not after an ordinal such as "1300."), parallel to the subtitle sentence; the 8-gram subsample uses a stable 64-bit key built from two CRC32s, the cheaper of the two stable hashes the proposal named; the subtitle code-page repairs aren't applied to Wikipedia, which is native UTF-8. Tokens with a digit, and runs of letters outside the language's inventory, are replaced by a gap marker (⟂) rather than dropping the sentence, because in YUE 35% of prose sentences contain a digit (42% of characters) against 2–4.5% of subtitle lines; how gaps are treated is open (STATUS.md).

2026-10-08 — YUE Wikipedia sample: all 135 row groups, 134,140 articles; 7,021 without a prose line, 30,760 dropped at template share 0.5 (23%), 96,359 kept with 534,344 sentences and 19.9M characters (57.0 MB; 55.9 MB at the 0.3 arm). 42% of kept sentences contain a gap: dropping them keeps 10.9M characters (55%); splitting at them gives 992,312 segments averaging 20 characters.

2026-10-08 — **[post hoc, filters]** Wikipedia sentence splitting, before any entropy. The first splitter cut sentences after ordinals: a digit before the full stop ("1300. urtean", "13. März") was handled before the first Basque run, but Roman-numeral ordinals and initials weren't ("XIX. mendean", "II. Mundu Gerran", "J. R. R. Tolkien"): 15,101 cut sentences in the Basque sample, 14,832 after a Roman numeral. A piece that ends in a number, a Roman numeral or a single capital followed by a full stop is now rejoined to the next. This matters to the pre-stated isolating-versus-agglutinative contrast, since ordinal dots occur in EUS, FIN, HUN and TUR (and DEU, SRP) but not in VIE, CMN, YUE or THA (a side agent raised the unevenness). The Basque Wikipedia sample is discarded and re-fetched.

2026-10-08 — The approved stub rule fails outside CJK. On the first Basque run (225,347 articles from 227 row groups) it dropped 219,674 articles (97.5%): in an alphabetic script an 8-character window is about a word and a half, so nearly every window occurs in 20 or more articles of a large sample. In YUE a character is about a syllable and the rule behaved as measured. Proposed revision, awaiting Brett: 8-character windows where a character is roughly a syllable (CMN, YUE, JPN, KOR), 24-character windows elsewhere (Latin scripts, Thai); every other part of the rule unchanged. On the Basque cache, 24-character windows drop 52% of articles and 25% of prose at 0.5 (56% and 25% at 0.3), and word 4-grams give nearly the same (52%, 24%). The dropped articles sampled are bot templates (species, places in the United States, English parishes, minerals, German municipalities); those under 0.2 are human-written. Until Brett decides, pass 2 runs only for CMN, YUE, JPN and KOR (`PASS2_OK` in `src/stage2_wikipedia.py`).

## Decisions approved by Brett, 2026-10-08 (evening)

2026-10-08 — Stub rule revision approved (Brett): template-share windows are 8 characters for CMN, YUE, JPN and KOR, where a character is roughly a syllable, and 24 characters for the Latin-script languages and Thai. The rest of the rule stands (prose lines, digits masked, 1 in 4 kept by a stable hash, 20-article floor, 0.5 main and 0.3 second arm). Decision owner: Brett.

2026-10-08 — Wikipedia gap treatment approved (Brett): main arm splits sentences at each gap (a removed token with a digit or out-of-inventory letters), so no context crosses it; second arm drops every sentence containing a gap, the rule the subtitles were cleaned under. Both reported. Decision owner: Brett.

2026-10-08 — Training-budget plan approved (Brett): (a) main analysis at the common budget B set by the smallest language; (b) the planned second run at B/2; (c) a run at 3B for every language that reaches it, with (a) recomputed on that subset so budget and language set aren't confounded; (d) the plateau check reported per language and rung, a failing rung flagged as data-limited, not dropped. The Wikipedia run is a further check at a different budget; Basque isn't set apart. Decision owner: Brett.

2026-10-08 — Stage 3 unit-convention rule and the Japanese and Thai plans approved (Brett) as written in `notes/stage3-proposal-units-jpn-tha.md` (commit df7d182, 2026-10-08 20:50 EDT, before any pipeline syllable count or NS ratio): grouping conventions chosen by the median per-text ratio to NS on primary texts, ties within 0.01 kept as arms, the ratio then reported as a calibration; accent and stress labels settled by the Stage 4 ShE comparison (WebCelex with and without stress for ENG and DEU); research-question forks run at every rung, unit conventions get one main convention plus a sensitivity row for the headline R_k. Decision owner: Brett.

2026-10-08 — **[post hoc, filters]** Vietnamese: some subtitle files write Đ/đ with the look-alike eth Ð/ð (U+00D0, U+00F0), so the letter check dropped valid lines ("Ðể sau đi."): 43 of 300 sampled letter rejects, about 450 of 5.25M lines, plus 3 files lost to the letter-rate rule. Eth is now mapped to d-bar for VIE in both fetchers (Vietnamese has no eth), and the VIE subtitles are re-fetched so the sample comes from one code version.

2026-10-08 — Cantonese cleaning in the main comparison (Brett; option C): YUE enters the main subtitle comparison under the drop rule (every sentence containing a gap dropped, 10.9M characters), the rule applied to the 16 subtitle languages; YUE under the split rule (19.9M characters) is reported as a sensitivity row. The all-Wikipedia run keeps split as main and drop as second arm for all 17. Raised by a side agent. Decision owner: Brett.

2026-10-08 — Budget with and without YUE (Brett): the main curve with YUE and the curve without YUE use the same common budget B, set by the smallest language including YUE, so the two curves differ only in whether YUE is in them. Decision owner: Brett.

2026-10-08 — Thai Wikipedia prose-line rule approved (Brett), from two random row groups (36,782 lines): a line is prose if it has at least 80 Thai characters and doesn't begin with an infobox marker (| { }), which keeps 73% of Thai characters (lines under 40 characters are headings, categories, infobox fragments and taxon names; 40 to 80 mostly list items; 80 and up mostly prose). Prose lines are split at spaces, Thai's sentence and clause break, into the units used as lines. Sensitivity row: the same at 150 Thai characters (62% of Thai characters), written separately as `THA-p150` with template shares recomputed on that subset. Thai units will be shorter than other languages' sentences, since Thai also spaces around conjunctions in lists; mean unit length is reported. Decision owner: Brett.

2026-10-08 — **[post hoc, filters]** Hungarian: ô also stands in for ő in some subtitle files ("kôrházban"), 72 of 300 sampled letter rejects, about 980 of 6.94M lines (0.01%); mapped ô→ő for HUN (Hungarian has no ô) and HUN re-fetched after VIE, as for the Vietnamese eth. Policy from here on: a look-alike or code-page residue that costs under 0.1% of a language's lines is logged as known residue, not fixed by re-fetching; fixes above that threshold are re-fetched.

2026-10-08 — Letter-check losses measured from the subtitle manifests (lines dropped by the letter check in kept files; sentences in files dropped by the 20% letter-rate rule): CAT 0.23%/0; DEU 0.36%/0.57% (50 files); ENG 0.31%/0.08%; EUS 0.07%/0; HUN 0.06%/0.02%; SRP 0.08%/0.05%; TUR 0.09%/0.00%; VIE 0.06%/0.04%; JPN 1.90%, KOR 1.93%, THA 0.78%, CMN 9.41% (mostly genuine Latin-script text, such as English halves of bilingual subtitles). German's files are double-encoded UTF-8 ("drÃŒben" for "drüben"). Not repaired (an `ftfy` pass would mean re-fetching all 16 languages): the damage depends on the uploader's software, not the text, and the 200 MB cap replaced dropped files with later films in the shuffled order, so no language is short and the sample isn't skewed. The re-fetch policy above is sharpened accordingly: re-fetch when a loss depends on content or leaves a language short of its cap, not on a percentage alone. Brett may override.

2026-10-08 — **[post hoc, sampling]** One file per film, enforced across year folders. An integrity check of every sample (`src/stage2_check.py`) found folders taken twice in CMN, DEU, FIN, ITA and KOR. Two causes: OPUS files a subtitle without an IMDb id under the placeholder folder 4294967295 (2^32 − 1), so unrelated films share it, one per year folder; and a film is sometimes filed under two years (FIN 673507_66701_1_17 under 1971 and 1972). The sampling rule now skips the placeholder folder (film identity unknown, so neither one file per film nor the film-level split can be guaranteed) and any film id already taken, the first in the existing shuffled order winning. The shuffle order is unchanged, so languages with neither case in their sample are byte-identical under the new rule (EUS, FRA, SPA, SRP, THA, TUR, VIE); the eight with placeholders or repeats (CMN, DEU, ENG, FIN, HUN, ITA, JPN, KOR) and CAT (interrupted mid-re-fetch) are re-fetched. EUS, CAT and JPN were also re-fetched under the final normaliser, since their first samples predate the entity, private-use and sara am changes.

## Restated for the public record (2026-10-09)

Decisions whose original entries are withheld from the public record, because those entries mix them with correspondence, are restated here without it. The originals are in the full record timestamped 2026-10-09 (Bitcoin block 970616); these restatements are dated today, still before any ratio to NS.

<!-- cleared: restated without correspondence 2026-10-09 -->
2026-10-09 — Restatement of the Stage 3 convention rule (approved 2026-10-08; `notes/stage3-proposal-units-jpn-tha.md`, §1). Conventions that change how segments or morae group into syllables (Japanese long vowels, the moraic nasal, geminates, vowel sequences; Korean resyllabification; French schwa) change a text's syllable count, so they are chosen against NS, the CSV's count of syllables in each text's canonical pronunciation (paper, Methods; one value per language and text, checked on all 255). For each language with two or more candidate groupings, the ratio of pipeline syllables to NS is computed on each primary text; the main convention is the one whose median ratio is closest to 1, and every candidate's median and per-text range is reported; candidates within 0.01 of the best are both kept as arms; for a language where NS chose the convention, its ratio is reported as a calibration, not as an independent check of unit consistency. Conventions that only label syllables (pitch accent, lexical stress) leave the count unchanged and are decided in Stage 4 against the published ShE, with WebCelex's stress marks for ENG and DEU.

<!-- cleared: restated without correspondence 2026-10-09 -->
2026-10-09 — Restatement of the Stage 3 scope of the unit-consistency check (decided 2026-10-08). Digits occur in 10 of the 15 read texts (phone numbers such as 762 584, clock times, dates, house numbers, counts), and Latin letters in YUE (O1 *D*, *la*; P8 *bing*, *bang*, *o*) and once in JPN (P3 *L*). The paper doesn't say how such tokens were read when NS was counted, and choosing readings by comparing counts with NS would fit the check to its own target. The check therefore runs both ways: primary, each language's texts with no digit and, for CMN, YUE, JPN, KOR and THA, no Latin letter (7 to 15 texts per language; P1, P9, Q0 and Q1 qualify in all 17); secondary, all 15 texts, with digits and letters expanded by reading conventions fixed per language before any count is compared with NS. The per-language ratio of pipeline syllables to NS is reported for both.

<!-- cleared: restated without correspondence 2026-10-09 -->
2026-10-09 — Restatement of the read-text entry (2026-10-08). The 255 read texts (15 × 17) are extracted from SM Text S3 to `data/ref/texts.tsv` by `src/extract_texts.py`, with two repairs of PDF artefacts: line-wrap spaces removed in CMN, YUE and JPN, and Thai sara am rebuilt where the extraction split it. Since NS counts the syllables of the canonical pronunciation, digits and Latin letters in the texts need spoken forms before counting (scope above).

## Stage 3: G2P agreement check (2026-10-09, before running)

2026-10-09 — Before choosing a main G2P for each Latin-script language, a check of how well the candidate tools agree on syllable counts: 100 word tokens per language, sampled with seed 20261009 from that language's subtitle sample (reservoir sample of 2,000 lines, then tokens of letters only, lower-cased). Methods: Epitran (not ENG, EUS), espeak-ng 1.52.0, CMUdict for ENG, pyphen hyphenation where a dictionary exists (eu, ca, de, en, fr, it, es, sr, hu). For the G2P outputs a syllable is a maximal run of vowel symbols (length marks attached; for SRP also r between consonants or between a word edge and a consonant); for CMUdict, the stress-marked vowels; for pyphen, hyphenation parts plus one. Reported: pairwise rates of exact agreement in syllable count. It compares tools with one another and touches no NS value; grouping conventions such as diphthong against hiatus are still decided by the approved NS rule, so disagreements that come only from vowel sequences are reported as such. VIE is skipped (written syllables are syllables).

2026-10-09 — **[post hoc, diagnostic]** Corrections to the G2P agreement check after its first run, which concern only how the check counts and touch no NS value: (1) a consonant carrying the syllabic mark (U+0329, as in Epitran's German -en, anfaŋn̩) counts as a nucleus; (2) pyphen runs with left=1, right=1, since its default typesetting minimums never break after a word's first letter or before its last (eta, una came out as one piece); (3) the logged wording "hyphenation parts plus one" was wrong: syllables are the number of pieces, which is what the code counts. First-run rates: EUS espeak–pyphen 79; CAT 99/87/88; DEU 83/82/95; ENG 98/92/90; FRA 95/93/94; ITA 97/96/95; SPA 100/90/90; SRP 100/93/93; TUR 98; FIN 100; HUN 99/91/92 (per 100 words).

2026-10-09 — **[post hoc, diagnostic]** Two findings from the corrected check. (1) Epitran's `srp-Latn` map (lines 31–32 of its `srp-Latn.csv`) writes every l and n with U+0329, the IPA syllabic mark (dole → dol̩e, nema → n̩ema), apparently meant as the dental mark U+032A; the check ignores U+0329 in SRP, and if Epitran becomes the main Serbian G2P the pipeline must strip it. (2) pyphen with left=1, right=1 isn't a usable syllabifier for English (you → y-ou, her → h-er); for ENG the pair is CMUdict and espeak-ng. Results: `results/stage3/g2p_check_summary.tsv`.

2026-10-09 — r3's proof is complete: attested in Bitcoin block 970627, mined 2026-10-09 12:21:03 UTC (Merkle root 274354d705b8aa22ca4129d7ab7186d1c2c936de9b9d4c67539f079f89ffdae8, checked against blockstream.info). The NS hold lifts only where the candidate conventions are already approved and in a timestamped snapshot: JPN and THA (`notes/stage3-proposal-units-jpn-tha.md`, in r3). For the other 15 languages no pipeline runs on the read texts until their candidate lists are approved and timestamped (side agent): otherwise a candidate could be added or dropped after seeing which matches NS. Testing on corpus words stays allowed.

2026-10-09 — **Correction to the G2P agreement check** (side agent; Brett: "Gelman wouldn't be happy with a 95% cutoff"). Its counter takes every maximal run of vowel symbols as one nucleus, so it counts Finnish *teos* and Hungarian *fiú* as one syllable whichever tool transcribed them (Hungarian *koreográfia* came out 4, against 6 with each vowel a nucleus). It can't see diphthong against hiatus, the ambiguity the candidate lists decide, and catches a vowel-sequence difference only where one tool writes a glide. The rates say the tools agree on where vowels and consonants are, nothing more; commit bf25332's message "tools agree on syllable counts" overstates them. No agreement rate certifies a tool: a 5-in-100 word difference can be systematic (FRA's 5 are espeak-ng's schwa deletions), and a systematic few-percent shift in counts exceeds the 0.01 tie band of the NS rule. From here: the main G2P per language is fixed from what each tool gets wrong on named test words, not from these rates; each candidate grouping gets its own counter; the second G2P's counts are reported beside the main one's, not used to select.

2026-10-09 — Stage 3 candidate conventions for the other 15 languages proposed (`notes/stage3-candidate-conventions.md`), before any read-text run for them. Main G2Ps are fixed from tool errors on named test and corpus words (Epitran's Catalan map writes high vocoids as consonants; its Spanish loses stressed hiatus; its German misreads *Museum*, *Ruine*; espeak-ng's Italian glides are inconsistent). French is left open: Epitran `fra-Latn` miscounts verb -ent, -aient and *quatrième*-type words on 64 frequent subtitle words, and espeak-ng gets them right but applies its own schwa deletion, so neither builds both candidates (options in the note; Lexique 3.80 recommended if it marks schwa). g2pk2 kept the Hangul block count on 300 Korean subtitle lines. Awaiting Brett.

A side agent then pointed out that the read-only sandbox blocks writes, not reads, and the payload sat inside the project, so NS (`data/ref/`) and the folder holding the correspondence with the paper's authors were readable. Audit of the transcript: all 39 commands ran in the payload folder with paths inside it; outside it, only Codex's auto-loaded 3-line `AGENTS.md` and a Codex skill file were read. No command touched `data/ref/`, `data/raw/`, the correspondence folder or the read texts, so the NS hold is intact and the correspondence wasn't read. From here, external-model payloads are staged outside any project repository (scratchpad), and each run's transcript is audited for reads outside the payload before its output is used; staging limits what an agent finds by browsing, it doesn't stop a read by absolute path.

2026-10-09 — Approved in principle by Brett ("1-3 yes"), before any ratio to NS exists in any language: (1) the selection rule becomes B+ (a calibration default chosen by distance from 1, the tie band measured that way, the candidate with the smallest typical per-text discrepancy also retained; every candidate gets the full headline curve; information rate reported both as the paper computes it and as a matched-count rate; cancellation measured, not assumed), replacing option D, whose cancellation premise Sol's second review showed false (`private/reviews/sol-gelman-20261009-131804/`); (2) the candidate-list corrections and additions (ITA-C, EUS-C, a narrow CAT-B from the passage, Finnish anchor, class and combination, the Lexique check); (3) R_k on SDs of logs, as spec-review item 4. The wording is drafted in `notes/stage3-amendment-selection-rule.md` for Brett to read; it takes effect when he has read it and it is logged and timestamped. JPN and THA stay paused until then.

2026-10-09 — Multiverse design (Brett: "can we just do a multiverse?", then "yes"). Instead of choosing a convention per language, every reasonable combination of the analysis forks is run and the headline is reported as its spread, with NS calibration as a reported lens; no default and no selection rule. Supersedes the B+ wording drafted earlier today (`notes/stage3-amendment-selection-rule.md`), which is not logged; the approved candidate-list changes and R_k on SDs of logs carry over. Published pronunciation dictionaries enter as further options (the paper's own sources first: WebCelex for ENG and DEU, Lexique 3.80 for FRA, E-Hitz for EUS; then PhonItalia, and EsPal if it gives syllabified forms), each with its terms and size checked before any download. The scope is fixed after a timing benchmark of the estimator on synthetic data or characters, so that no syllable entropy exists before the stamp. Specification to follow for Brett's reading.

2026-10-09 — The multiverse specification's eight open choices, decided by Brett one by one (`notes/multiverse-spec.md` §9): (1) the YUE gap treatment is a plain fork, drop and split both options; (2) a language's options share the same text, not the same token count; (3) cross-language combinations enumerated up to 10^6, otherwise 10^5 drawn uniformly with a fixed seed; (4) Modal first if the full crossing won't fit locally in time or under the 40 GB disk floor, after OpenSubtitles' terms are checked for cloud processing, with the declared cut order only as a fallback; (5) FIN-C (three exact words) for calibration only; (6) the NS lens is a plot of R_k against calibration score, with no cut-off subset; (7) named universes are first-listed, dictionary and NS-closest, and the extremes get no intervals; (8) KenLM is the estimator, after a fake-data check that it recovers a known entropy at each order. Decision owner: Brett.

2026-10-09 — Fake data first (Brett: "we should do fake data before actually running right?"). The whole pipeline runs on simulated data with known answers before any syllable entropy of a real corpus or any ratio to NS: the estimator's recovery of known conditional entropies (F1), the effect of regrouping units where the answer is known (F2), the full Stage 6 analysis on seventeen fake languages under a surviving and a vanishing compression scenario, with interval coverage over repeated simulations (F3), and the runner's plumbing (F4); `notes/multiverse-spec.md` §3a. Results go to Brett before any real run. Grounding: Gelman et al. (2020), *Bayesian workflow*, §4.1.

2026-10-09 — **[F1, fake data]** First findings, on simulated text only. (1) KenLM can't compute its closed-form modified Kneser–Ney discounts on these data: "Could not calculate Kneser-Ney discounts for 1-grams with adjusted count 2 because we didn't observe any 1-grams with adjusted count 1" (lmplz, every order). A small closed inventory has no rare types: every type follows many different types. Real syllable inventories are small and closed, so the same failure is expected there; F1 runs with `--discount_fallback` (fixed discounts where the estimate fails) and records it. Whether to use the fallback or another estimator on the real data is a modelling decision for Brett. (2) Where extra context carries no information, the estimate still rises with order (lag-1 copy process, 600 types: about 0.06, 0.12 and 0.18 bits above the truth at k = 2, 3 and 4 with 5M tokens; 0.04, 0.08 and 0.13 with 30M). The full grid is still running. Added at a side agent's suggestion: F1b, the same check with lines of 15 tokens, for the line-bounded mode, where context is cut at each line and the end-of-line probability lowers every other token's probability (`src/fakedata/f1b_short_lines.py`).

The anchored proofs were pushed to the public repository (commit 00e9fc4).

2026-10-09 — **[F1b, fake data]** With lines of 15 tokens, the unigram estimate is 0.07–0.11 bits above the truth, about the cost of one token in 16 being an end of line (−log2(15/16) ≈ 0.09). Dependencies across intervening tokens are largely missed: for a lag-4 copy process, the estimate at k = 4 is 2.5, 3.3 and 4.0 bits above the truth with 600, 2,000 and 10,000 types, and for lag 2 at k = 2 it is 0.3, 0.8 and 1.6 bits above (`results/fakedata/f1b_short_lines.tsv`). An n-gram model uses a context only if it has seen it, and its backoff drops the most distant token first; the failure grows with the number of distinct contexts, and so with inventory size. KenLM substituted fallback discounts only for the unigrams ("Substituting fallback discounts for order 0"); higher orders' discounts were estimated, so the bias is the estimator's, not the fallback's. These processes are deliberately adversarial; what matters for the research question is whether estimator bias that grows with inventory size moves R_k, which is for F3.

2026-10-09 — **[F1, fake data, complete]** 180 models (lag-m copy processes, m = 1–4; 600, 2,000 and 10,000 types; 5M, 10M and 30M training tokens; lines of 1,000; `results/fakedata/f1_estimator.tsv`). Bias at k = m, the order that should capture the dependency, in bits: m = 1, 0.01–0.05; m = 2, 0.11–2.00; m = 3, 1.21–4.29; m = 4, 2.82–5.25, larger with more types and falling only slowly with more data (m = 2 with 2,000 types: 0.94, 0.74 and 0.46 at 5M, 10M and 30M). Extra orders beyond the dependency also cost bits (m = 1 at k = 4: 0.13–0.23). KenLM recovers adjacent dependence well; it misses most of a dependence that skips over unrelated tokens, because it can use a context only if it has seen it. For the research question, this means held-out n-gram cross-entropy at k ≥ 2 measures the information an n-gram model can pick up from budget B, which depends on inventory size and data, and not only on the language's conditional entropy. Whether that moves R_k is the question for F3; a second estimator that generalises over unseen contexts (the spec's optional neural syllable LM) is a candidate fork for Brett.

2026-10-09 — **[F1, corrections from Sol's review]** (`private/reviews/sol-estimator-20261009-160603/`, checked against the files). (1) The earlier entry's "the bias is the estimator's, not the fallback's" overstated it: four F1 models ran without the fallback (lag 2, 10,000 types, 5M tokens) and still overshoot by 2.0 bits at k = 2, so the fallback isn't needed for the large errors, but a change at the lowest order propagates through interpolation and its contribution hasn't been measured. Also, which levels used the fallback rests on one diagnostic run; the F1 scripts kept only a yes/no flag and discarded the retry's stderr. (2) The extra-order cost at k = 4 for lag 1 is 0.13–0.27 bits, not 0.13–0.23 (10,000 types was left out). (3) F1's reference entropy ignores the context reset at each 1,000-token line; the correct reference at k ≥ m is H_copy + (m/L)(H(p) − H_copy), a difference of 0.015–0.020 bits at lag 4, too small to matter for the multi-bit errors. (4) F1 completed its grid but has not "passed" anything: no tolerance was declared, each cell is one realisation, and the test size grows with the training size. These go into the next version of F1 before F3.

2026-10-09 — F3b and thresholds (Brett). Fake text for F3b: the local family (dependence between adjacent syllables, varying strength) is the main family for reading F3b; F1's skipped-dependency processes are a stress test (Brett: "Local text only", from the options put to him). No pass/fail bar for F3 (Brett asked whether Gelman would like thresholds and what he would do): a threshold declared in advance protects decisions taken after real results, while F3 is fake data, where seeing results and changing the design is the purpose of the step (Gelman et al. 2020, §4.1), as long as every change is made and logged before any real estimate; cf. Gelman & Stern (2006) on the arbitrariness of thresholds. F3a: the zero-noise test checks the code; bias and coverage are reported with Monte Carlo error. F3b: distortion reported as estimated against true, and against the S1–S2 contrast; the second estimator decided afterwards on design and cost, before any real estimate; F3b's distortions reported beside the real results, not subtracted. Supersedes the proposed 0.01 / 85–95% / 0.05 bars (`notes/multiverse-spec.md` §3a).

2026-10-09 — **[F3a, fake data, true densities]** 500 replicates per scenario and experiment (`results/fakedata/f3a_oracle.tsv`). Fixed panel: bias in R_k −0.006 to +0.016 (from sampling 10 speakers per language, which puts measurement error in the language-mean SR), RMSE about 0.04; the language bootstrap's 90% interval covers every time but is 0.27–0.52 wide, so for the 17 languages as given it is very conservative. Redrawn panels, the population target the bootstrap claims: coverage 0.85–0.88 against a nominal 0.90 (Monte Carlo error about ±0.013), bias +0.03 to +0.05 at R ≈ 0.6, RMSE 0.15–0.23. The change from compression within words to none across words (0.737) is recovered with bias −0.02 to −0.03. Reading: R_k as a population quantity is imprecise with 17 languages; the large contrast between surviving and disappearing compression is recoverable, smaller ones would sit inside that noise. Simulation artefact, not an analysis bug: in S0 and S1 every rung has the same simulated pattern, so the paired differences are exactly zero and their "coverage" turns on floating-point ties (0.85 against 1.0); the next F3a run gives each rung its own small variation.

2026-10-09 — Both intervals reported, each for its own target (Brett: "why wouldn't we report both?"). For these 17 languages: a bootstrap over speakers within each language and texts jointly across languages (they are translations), and for real data the corpus documents behind ID, using the basic (bias-reflecting) interval; added to `src/stage6/analysis.py` (`panel_bootstrap`, `basic_interval`) with a zero-noise test. For languages in general: the bootstrap over languages, labelled as treating the 17 as a sample from a population, with a leave-one-family-out check. **[F3a, rerun]** with each rung given its own variation (`results/fakedata/f3a_oracle.tsv`): fixed panel, the panel interval is about 0.12 wide against 0.28–0.65 for the language interval; the percentile panel interval covered 78–87% and only 53–60% for a large paired change, because sampling 10 speakers per language biases R_k (+0.015 at R ≈ 0.6) and a percentile interval carries the bias; the basic interval covers 84–88% throughout, including 86–88% for the large paired change. Redrawn panels: the panel interval covers 25–37%, as expected, since it ignores which languages were sampled; the language interval covers 81–88%. Both run a few points under nominal; reported with their simulated coverage rather than adjusted.

2026-10-09 — **[F1 version 2, short lines and ablation]** (`results/fakedata/f1v2_short.tsv`, `f1v2_ablation.tsv`; long lines still running). (1) The fallback ablation: KenLM used each declared setting at the unigram level (its logs show "Substituting fallback discounts for order 0: D1=0.25 D2=0.5 D3+=0.75" and so on), while the higher orders' estimated discounts were identical across settings; test and validation losses agree to the fifth decimal at every order for lag 1 and lag 2 with 2,000 types. With a small closed inventory every type has thousands of distinct predecessors, so discounts of 0.25 to 2 move each unigram probability by about one part in ten thousand. On data like these the fallback values don't matter, including through interpolation, which answers the concern in Sol's estimator review. (2) Short lines (15 tokens, 10M, three seeds): the end-of-line normalisation removes the line-end cost (unigram bias raw about +0.09 bits, normalised within ±0.005). Against the declared tolerance (normalised; k ≤ 1; independent and lag-1 processes): every cell passes except independent draws with 10,000 types at k = 1 (+0.086 bits; seeds 0.082–0.088), the cost of an order that carries nothing, on a large inventory with truncated context; reported as it stands. KenLM fell back at the unigram level only in all 225 short-line models. (3) Plug-in entropy on the training sample, the direction Dunning's rare-event warning points: at k = 0 within ±0.006 bits; at k = 1 below the truth by 0.02–0.03 bits with 600 types, 0.10–0.17 with 2,000, and 0.29–0.62 with 10,000. A within-word plug-in such as the paper's ID therefore errs low by an amount that grows with inventory size relative to the data.

2026-10-09 — **The F1 version 2 tolerance failed, and the earlier entry passed over it** (side agent). The rule declared before the run (`notes/multiverse-spec.md` §3a) said: at k ≤ 1 for the independent and lag-1 processes, within 0.05 bits at every budget, and "failing that, the scorer or the setup is wrong and is fixed before anything else". It failed for independent draws with 10,000 types at k = 1: +0.086 bits with lines of 15 (three seeds, 0.082–0.088), and also with lines of 1,000 at 5M and 10M tokens (+0.110 and +0.089; seed 1), which the earlier entry didn't mention; 2,000 types at 5M came close (+0.047). Calling it "reported as it stands" did not follow the rule. Diagnosis, done now as the rule required (and before F3b's results are read, since F3b uses the same scorer): the scorer agrees exactly with KenLM's own `query` tool (consistency check at the start of every run); the unigram is within ±0.007 bits at every budget and inventory; and the excess at k = 1 shrinks with training data (10,000 types: 0.110, 0.089, 0.062 at 5M, 10M, 30M) and grows with inventory size (600 types: 0.023 to 0.008; 2,000: 0.047 to 0.017). That is the cost of estimating an order whose context carries no information, when the inventory is large for the data, not a fault in the scorer or setup.

2026-10-09 — **[post hoc] Proposed revision of that tolerance, for Brett to confirm.** The k = 1 part of the rule assumed an uninformative extra order costs nothing, and it doesn't. Revised check: the scorer and setup are judged by the unigram (within 0.01 bits at every budget) and by agreement with KenLM's `query`; at k ≥ 1, the excess for an order that carries no information is reported as estimator cost, with the requirement that it fall as the budget grows. Under the revision, F1 version 2 passes (so far: every unigram within ±0.007; every excess at k = 1 falling with budget). Made after seeing the results, so marked post hoc; the original rule and its failure stay in the record.

2026-10-09 — **Corrections to the two entries above** (Sol's review of the revision, `private/reviews/sol-revision-20261009-174144/`, checked against the files). (1) "The unigram is within ±0.007 bits at every budget and inventory" is false; it was read off the independent-draw cells only. Within the rule's own scope (independent and lag-1 processes) six lag-1 unigram cells fall outside ±0.01 (−0.017 to +0.017), and the lag-1 bigram with 10,000 types at 5M is +0.051, also over 0.05; the independent bigram with 10,000 types fails at 30M as well (+0.062), not only at 5M and 10M. Outside that scope, unigram cells of the other processes also exceed ±0.01. (2) The unigram misses are largely test-sample variation: scored with the true probabilities on the same 1M test tokens, one failing cell (short lines, lag 1, 600 types, seed 3) is already +0.0117 from the population value against +0.0120 reported, so a ±0.01 requirement on one correlated million-token test set can reject even oracle scoring. The independent-bigram excess survives that comparison (fitted minus same-test oracle about 0.09 bits at 10,000 types), so for it the estimator-cost reading stands. (3) "Agrees exactly with query" overstates the consistency check: it compares raw scores on one 600-type trigram model and doesn't test the normalised scores or the failing configurations. (4) "Under the revision, F1 version 2 passes" is false: the revision kept a unigram ±0.01 check that fails, and the long-line grid is incomplete. (5) Continuing to F3b while the declared stop-and-fix instruction was unmet departs from that instruction; F3b is diagnostic until the checks below are done. The proposed revision of the earlier entry is withdrawn in favour of the one put to Brett next.

2026-10-09 — **[post hoc] Revision of the F1 checks, confirmed by Brett ("carry on"), for work after this date.** Replacing the withdrawn proposal, on Sol's design: three separate checks. (1) Is the code right: raw scores agree with KenLM's `query` on representative configurations, including the failing ones, and normalised scores agree with an explicit sum over all syllable outcomes at sampled positions. (2) How much does the estimator cost: each cell reports the discrepancy from the population reference, the same-test oracle discrepancy (the test tokens scored with the true probabilities), and fitted minus oracle, with sampling variation across seeds; the cost is compared with an independent reconstruction of modified Kneser–Ney from the counts for a failing bigram configuration; falling with budget is a diagnostic, not a pass condition. (3) Does it matter downstream: F3b, read as estimated against true, under Brett's earlier decision that F3 has no pass/fail bar. No numerical bound is set from results already seen. The original F1 version 2 rule stays recorded as failed; this revision doesn't make it a pass. F3b continues as a diagnostic and is read after checks (1) and (2).

2026-10-09 — **[F1, check 2: same-test oracle]** `src/fakedata/f1_oracle.py` scores each cell's test tokens with the true probabilities (`results/fakedata/f1v2_short_oracle.tsv`; long lines `f1v2_long_oracle_partial.tsv`, 530 rows so far). Unigram: the oracle itself sits −0.022 to +0.017 bits from the population value (test-sample variation in a correlated million-token test set), and the fitted unigram stays within 0.009 bits of the oracle everywhere, so the unigram misses of the original rule were sampling, not the scorer. At k = 1, fitted minus oracle (the estimator's own cost): independent draws 0.006–0.021 bits with 600 types, 0.024–0.055 with 2,000, 0.065–0.113 with 10,000; lag 1, 0.004–0.012, 0.013–0.026 and 0.029–0.048; all falling with budget. Checks 1a, 1b and the reconstruction are running.

2026-10-09 — **[F1, checks 1 and 2 complete]** (`results/fakedata/f1_checks.md`, `src/fakedata/f1_checks.py`). (1a) Raw scores from the Python scorer agree with KenLM's `query` within 1.5 × 10^−9 bits, with identical token counts, on five configurations including those that failed the original rule (independent draws with 10,000 types at 5M and lines of 1,000, and at 10M with lines of 15; lag 1 with 10,000 types; lag 2 with 2,000 types, lines of 15, order 3; lag 1 with 600 types, order 5). (1b) At 300 sampled positions per model, the probabilities over all syllables plus end of line and unknown sum to 1 within 1.5 × 10^−7, and the code's normaliser equals the explicit sum within the same margin. (2) Modified Kneser–Ney rebuilt from the counts for the worst bigram case (independent draws, 10,000 types, 5M, lines of 1,000) matches KenLM token by token within 5.4 × 10^−7 in log10 probability and in cross-entropy within 3 × 10^−9 bits; the discounts computed from counts-of-counts (0.766969, 1.059253, 1.308236) equal those KenLM logged, and the unigram level used the fallback. The code is right, and the excess at k = 1 for large inventories is modified Kneser–Ney's own behaviour on these data, shown independently. F3b can now be read.

2026-10-09 — **[F3b, local family]** (`results/fakedata/f3b_local.tsv`; read after checks 1 and 2 passed). Seventeen fake languages with lag-1 copy processes whose true density is constant across k = 1–4 (4–6 bits), inventories 600–10,000, three pairings of inventory with density, 10M training tokens. KenLM's error in bits grows with k (mean +0.03 at k = 1 to +0.29 at k = 4, varying by language), but its effect on R_k is small: at most 0.037 in absolute value (S0, inventories opposed to density, k = 4), and at most 0.008 under the trade-off scenario S1. A largely common error mostly cancels in a measure of relative spread; what remains shrinks the spread of log ID slightly, which matters more without a trade-off (dR/dv = v/R ≈ 0.67 in S0, against (v + r)/R ≈ 0.16 in S1). With the truth constant, the estimate drifts by up to −0.04 from k = 1 to k = 4 in S0, so a real change in R_k across rungs smaller than about 0.04 would be confounded with the estimator; the sign at k = 1–2 depends on how inventory is paired with density (aligned +0.015 to +0.021, opposed −0.018 to −0.027). For scale: the contrast between surviving and disappearing compression in F3 is 0.74, the panel's sampling RMSE in F3a about 0.04, and the population RMSE 0.15–0.23. The skipped-dependency stress test is still running.

2026-10-09 — **[F3b, skipped family: the stress test]** (`results/fakedata/f3b_skipped.tsv`). Lag-3 copy processes: the true density falls at k = 3, where the dependency becomes visible, so the true R_k changes between k = 2 and 3. KenLM misses most of the dependency (0.5–3.6 bits too high at k = 3–4), so its R_k stays near the k = 1–2 value. Distortion at k = 3–4: with inventories independent of density, −0.09 to −0.10 without a trade-off and +0.38 with one (true 0.61, estimated 0.99); opposed, −0.27 and +0.71 (true 0.61, estimated 1.32); aligned, at most 0.05. Reading: where predictive information sits in dependencies that skip over intervening syllables, the n-gram estimate can hide a trade-off that emerges with longer context, or invent one, by as much as the contrast the research question turns on; with local dependence (the main family) it moves R_k by at most 0.04. Whether real cross-word information is local or skipping is unknown before the real analysis, so this bears on the second estimator, which Brett decides now that F3b is complete.

2026-10-09 — Second estimator: option B (Brett). Besides KenLM in every universe, the named universes (first-listed, dictionary, NS-closest) get a feed-forward neural model that sees exactly the k previous syllables, trained on the same text and budget, with early stopping on validation documents kept apart from the test documents and settings fixed on fake data. It must first pass F1 and F3b, and in particular recover dependencies that skip over intervening syllables, since that is where F3b's stress test showed KenLM can move R_k by up to 0.71; otherwise it adds nothing. Both curves are reported side by side (`notes/multiverse-spec.md` §2.2a). Reason: whether real cross-word information is local or skipping is unknown before the real analysis, and the two estimators bracket that question; deciding after seeing KenLM's real results would be a forking path.

2026-10-09 — The neural estimator's fake-data test gets a mapped process (side agent): the copy processes are the one long-range pattern that tied input and output embeddings make easy, while real long-range dependencies mostly link a syllable to a different one. In `src/fakedata/f1_mapped.py`, token t is a fixed random permutation σ of token t − m with probability 0.7, otherwise a fresh Zipf draw; the truth comes from the stationary distribution of each residue chain (checked against simulation with 20 types: H(π) 4.0157 against plug-in 4.0155, conditional 1.8225 against 1.8210, and the near-context entropy equal to H(π) at lag 3). Both estimators run on it at lag 1 and lag 3. The neural model enters only if it recovers the mapped dependency as well as the copied one; F3b's neural run gets a mapped stress family too.

2026-10-09 — **[neural F1, first 17 of 60 cells]** (`results/fakedata/f1_neural.tsv`, 600 types). Every cell ran the full three epochs (7,326 steps) with validation loss still falling, so early stopping never fired: the declared settings stop training too soon. Bias against the reference is +0.04 to +0.06 bits for independent draws, where the true model is a unigram, and +0.06 to +0.10 for the copy processes, growing with k. KenLM on the same cells (`f1v2_long.tsv`, 10M, seed 1) is better at k = 1 (+0.015 independent, +0.009 lag-1 copy) and much worse beyond it (+0.13 to +0.27 independent at k = 2–4; +1.60 and +1.70 for the lag-3 copy at k = 3–4, where the neural model has +0.08 and +0.09). So the neural model already recovers the skipped copy, but with a floor of about 0.05 bits that a converged model would not have. The floor is a failure of the settings fixed before the check, not yet of the model; the remaining cells (2,000 and 10,000 types) are left running to show how the gap scales with inventory. Any change to the settings is a design change made on fake data after seeing fake results, which the workflow allows if logged before any real estimate (§3a); it will be tried on seeds disjoint from the check's, and the check then rerun.

2026-10-09 — **[neural estimator: diagnosis on fake data, Codex task F]** On small CPU runs (100,000–1M tokens, tuning seeds disjoint from every check's), the floor is undertraining from a poor start, not a scorer error (the scorer agrees with a direct sum to 1.4e-7 bits, and setting the output bias to the true log probabilities reproduces the oracle). More epochs alone help slowly; untying the output doesn't help; initialising the output bias to the training text's log unigram frequencies, zeroing the context outputs and keeping the step-zero checkpoint removes the floor for independent draws (+0.0003 bits) but leaves the dependency cells still improving at the epoch cap. Four candidate settings (`baseline3`, `long4`, `unigram_cosine3`, `zero_unigram_cosine5`) go to a full-scale GPU sweep (80 fits, 10M tokens, about 6 hours), queued after the neural F1 run so that run's declared-settings record is complete. The choice among them is Brett's; after it, F1 (copy and mapped) and the neural F3b are rerun on their own seeds. The mapped-dependency check runs now with KenLM only (`f1_mapped.py --kenlm-only`); its neural half waits for the chosen settings, since the declared settings are known to stop short. The candidate code is under independent review and is not yet in the project.

2026-10-09 — Correction to the candidate note's DEU paragraph: it gave *leiten* as [ˈlaitn̩] and said it "gives two syllables in either transcription". Kohler (1990: 49) writes [ˈlaɪtn̩], and the note's R0 would count that string's unmarked [aɪ] as two nuclei, three syllables in all (Codex task D's report). The paragraph now gives the source's form, notes that [aɪ] is one of the three diphthongs the source lists, and says the count of two rests on the tool's tie (espeak-ng lˈa͡ɪtən, checked 2026-10-09). No option changes.

2026-10-09 — Independent read-only review of the tuning code (Codex, `private/reviews/codex-runs-20261009/review-F-20261009/`). No defect affects the numbers the GPU sweep will produce. One affects recovery: a row torn by a crash blocks resumption until it is removed by hand (the planned six-hour stop falls between fits, so it can't tear a row). For the settings decision: (a) the stopping rule counts an evaluation as an improvement only if validation falls by more than 1e-4 bits, so with the step-zero checkpoint a model still improving in smaller steps can keep step zero and stop; this rule is inherited from the declared settings, and its tolerance is a choice the spec doesn't make; (b) the mapped-process truth is the stationary entropy, while the simulator starts each chain from the Zipf marginal, which overstates the truth by at most 0.0004 bits on 50,000 test tokens and 0.00002 on a million; (c) the last 6–10 updates of each run (of 7,326–12,210) are never evaluated. The sweep runs as written.

2026-10-09 — **[F1, mapped process, KenLM]** (`results/fakedata/f1_mapped_kenlm.tsv`; lines of 1,000, 10M tokens). Lag 1: within 0.03 bits at k = 1, then +0.05 to +0.19 at k = 2–4, like the lag-1 copy. Lag 3: at k = 3–4, where the dependency becomes visible, KenLM is +3.47 to +3.51 bits off with 600 types, +5.28 to +5.31 with 2,000 and +6.78 to +6.79 with 10,000, against +1.60 to +1.70 for the lag-3 copy with 600 types. So an n-gram model recovers almost none of a skipped dependency between different syllables at these budgets. The neural half of this check waits for the chosen settings.

2026-10-09 — Review of the runner and assembly (Codex, read-only; `private/reviews/codex-runs-20261009/review-B-20261009/`): four defects (the manifest is written before the disk check, so the floor can be crossed; a cached job can be skipped after its input changes during a run; floating-point residue turns exact ties in R_4 − R_1w into rises; the estimate's identity includes metadata that doesn't affect the fit, forcing needless refits) and two unmet reporting requirements (matched-count results vanish silently when their input is missing; shared forks get no attribution of variation across settings). Sent back to Codex as task B2 with the review. Implementation choices given in that brief, as numerical or engineering matters only: a tie is |R_4 − R_1w| ≤ 1e-9 × max(1, |R_1w|), with rise, fall and tie shares reported separately; a 1 GiB margin above the floor before any write; pooling across shared settings defaults to equal weight per setting, parameterised and flagged as a choice for Brett.

2026-10-09 — Review of the syllable counters (Codex, read-only; `private/reviews/codex-runs-20261009/review-D-20261009/`): five defects despite 43 passing tests. The serious one: espeak-ng marks a word it reads in another language with labels such as `(en)` and `(de)`, and the counter parsed the labels' letters as vowels, so German *software* counted 4 and French *wifi* 3 (2 each without the labels). The others: Han characters outside the code's ranges counted 0; Unicode line separators didn't end phrases; a tie between syllabic consonants left two nuclei; FRA-B dropped Lexique's segmentation wherever a final schwa had been removed. The reviewer checked both fixture corrections the author made after failures and found each follows from the note. New open readings: Catalan function words, which espeak-ng stresses in isolation (so "unstressed" in CAT-B never applies to them); English words CMUdict lacks, where the predictor dropped accented letters (*café* 1, not 2); full-width Chinese punctuation, absent from the note's phrase-end list. Sent back as task D2, which also drafts a table of every reading the note leaves open, with its wording, the alternatives and words whose counts differ, for Brett.

- Row 1: full-width and CJK equivalents of the listed phrase-end marks end phrases (confirmed).

- Row 12: a frozen list of Catalan function words is treated as unstressed, so CAT-B's deletion of a word-final unstressed vowel applies to them despite the stress espeak-ng gives words in isolation.

- Row 13: a French spelling with several Lexique entries takes the pronunciation of the more prevalent one (Brett proposed a rule by prevalence; implemented with Lexique's frequency fields, to be confirmed on download).
- Row 15: an English word CMUdict lacks has its accents stripped and is looked up in CMUdict again, and only then goes to the g2p-en predictor.
- Row 16: SPA-B exempts hiatus with a stressed close vowel (*día*, *país*, *río* stay two syllables); within-word pairing otherwise as before.
- Row 18: ITA's list of lexically prescribed hiatus is built from English Wiktionary's Italian entries whose `it-IPA` respelling marks a hiatus with a dot (the convention's documentation: "must be indicated explicitly by placing a `.` between the vowels", with *biologia*), with provenance kept per entry.
- Rows 30, 31 and 33: Mandarin erhua comes from English Wiktionary (the "Mandarin erhua terms" category, 931 pages on 2026-10-10, and `zh-pron`'s `er` parameter, which records which syllable takes erhua), as a positional lexicon covering both 儿 and 兒, applied by longest match inside runs of Han characters; words Wiktionary records with variable syllable counts are marked as variable.
- Row 38: number readings postponed (no digits survive in the Stage 2 corpora; they matter only for the read texts' secondary run), with English Wiktionary to be considered as a source for writing numbers out.
Not yet decided: the other rows of the table, the neural settings (the 13-fit comparison is running), the design points of the brief's Part 3 (Brett asked for a fuller, simpler explanation), and the download of the Wiktionary extract the Wiktionary rows need.

2026-10-10 — Filter done: entries per Wiktionary language header in `data/interim/wiktionary/` (438 MB): English 1,492,836; Spanish 811,049; Italian 623,702; French 403,269; German 371,261; Chinese 327,297; Finnish 266,150; Catalan 198,491; Hungarian 78,817; Serbo-Croatian 70,309; Korean 63,773; Vietnamese 51,896; Turkish 45,949; Basque 18,243. The Italian and Chinese files go to the Codex working copy (public data; not committed there) to build the two lexicons Brett decided on. **For Brett to confirm (an operationalisation of his row-18 decision, by Claude):** the Italian list takes only entries whose IPA keeps an *unstressed i or u* syllabic beside a vowel, the case `it-IPA` marks explicitly with a dot, not every vowel sequence Wiktionary syllabifies, since Wiktionary splits all non-high sequences (*pa.é.se*) while the note's source treats those as one syllable inside a phrase and reserves two syllables for "lexically prescribed cases – typically where the sequence arises from adding a prefix to a stem" (Rogers & d'Arcangeli 2004: 119). Inflected forms enter only where Wiktionary gives them their own IPA; the gap is reported, not filled. For erhua, variable entries (花儿 huār and huā'ér) default to the erhua reading, recorded with each result.

Mandarin: 2,538 forms (1,932 after normalising 儿 and 兒), from 920 erhua-category members of which 47 had no usable Standard Mandarin pinyin; erhua is positional (兒媳婦兒 keeps its first 兒 as a syllable: 3; 哥兒們: 2; 花儿女儿: 3); 73 forms are variable and take the erhua reading. Italian: two corrections by Claude to the criterion logged above, **for Brett to confirm**. (1) A stress mark counts as a syllable break, as a dot does: Wiktionary writes the break before a stressed syllable as the stress mark (*rione* /riˈo.ne/, *viale*), and since it writes glides as j/w, a vowel-written unstressed i/u beside a vowel across either break is a hiatus; the dot-only version had missed these. (2) A pair is protected only if every Wiktionary pronunciation of the spelling has it, because the extract mixes homographs: *piano* is /ˈpja.no/ as noun, adverb and common adjective, but one rare adjective has /piˈa.no/, and the first version's union rule made every *piano* three syllables. The same rule lets the modern pronunciation win where Wiktionary also gives a traditional one (*liana*). Result: 2,713 Italian spellings protected (from 3,563 words with at least one hiatus pronunciation); inflected forms only where they have their own IPA (forms of *riaprire*, *riunire* and *riuscire* such as *riaperto*, *riunisco*, *riuscendo* lack it: a stated limitation).

- Row 25: kept; the book's list of 20 heterosyllabic combinations is added to the note.

Not marked: row 49 (who owns a syllable two words share; suggested as a fork).

2026-10-10 — Correction to the Italian hiatus criterion (Claude, after a side agent's note): Wiktionary's Italian IPA also splits falling sequences (*mai* /ˈma.i/, *voi*, *auto* /ˈa.u.to/, *suoi* /ˈswɔ.i/), which is its module's default, not a marked exception; the version logged earlier let the unstressed i/u come second, and so took *cui*, *lei*, *mai*, *auto* and other common words (0.92% of the Italian sample's tokens). The list now takes only an unstressed i or u *before* a vowel, the case `it-IPA` marks by hand: 2,039 spellings, 0.26% of the sample's tokens, the commonest *riesce*, *costruire*, *Luigi*, *influenza*, *statua*, *inviato*, *cliente*, *Trieste*, *Friuli*, *biografia* (branch `codex/t4-stage4-pipeline`, 6ff867f; every T4 test file passes). The other two corrections (stress mark as a break; a pair protected only if every pronunciation of the spelling has it) stand, still for Brett to confirm.

2026-10-10 — Brett: (1) **row 49 is a fork**: a syllable two words share (cross-word pairing, *la amica*) belongs to the left-hand word in one option and the right-hand word in the other, at the word-aware rungs; (2) **the three Italian hiatus criteria are confirmed**: a stress mark counts as a syllable break as a dot does; a pair is protected only if every Wiktionary pronunciation of the spelling has it; only an unstressed i or u *before* a vowel qualifies; (3) **Lexique 4.1**, downloaded from http://www.lexique.org/databases/Lexique410/Lexique4.1.zip (48,896,901 bytes, last modified 2026-09-29) into `data/raw/lexique/` (gitignored). Terms: the homepage's text names CC BY-SA 4.0 while its link goes to CC BY-NC 4.0; either allows this research use, and nothing derived from it is redistributed beyond what both permit. Requested citation: New, Pallier, Schalchli, Bourgin & Gimenes (2026), "Lexique 4: A major upgrade of the 'Lexique' French lexical database", *Behavior Research Methods* 58(5): 140. Lexique 3.80, the paper's version, is no longer available, so the original's French pronunciations can't be matched exactly (as the candidate note already states).

2026-10-10 — Lexique 4.1 arrived intact (48,896,901 bytes, SHA-256 d6d7b99801e1f0aeab90ed1623a83ba45b8333b6a646ee33f524a6c047ff4b23; `data/raw/lexique/`, gitignored). Its README gives the licence as CC BY-SA 4.0, which settles the homepage's mismatch. Columns used: `1_Mot`, `2_Phono` (the older Lexique alphabet), `3_Phono_IPA`, `10_FreqMot` (frequency per entry, i.e. per spelling, part of speech and lemma, from a 316-million-word subtitle corpus: the measure for row 13's rule by prevalence), `25_SyllPhono` (syllabified, hyphen-separated) and `26_SyllNb`. Finding that changes the note: of 189,863 entries, 30,910 mark an elidable schwa with ° but only one uses "3", the symbol the note gave for a non-elidable schwa; Lexique 4 writes non-elidable schwa as "2" (/ø/, *premier* pR2mje), the same symbol as the vowel ø. So FRA-A counts every vowel symbol including °, and FRA-B drops ° and keeps "2"; counts are as intended, and the note's FRA paragraph will say so. Row 14 (the alphabet) is checked by translating `2_Phono` with the code's mapping and comparing with `3_Phono_IPA` for every entry. Row 13: for a spelling with several entries, the one with the highest `10_FreqMot` (*couvent*: noun 5.237 against verb 0.082, so [kuvɑ̃], 2 syllables; *content*: adjective 102.794 against verb 0.291, 2).
